\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;.

(B.23)

Flipping the arrow introduces an overall minus sign since
|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle
is anti-symmetric.

Singlets in
fig.

6
are chained together by permutation
\pi
, forming paths between the gray circles (ignore the arrow directions for now). These paths effectively pair up the elements of the set
\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{\ell_{1}},y_{1},\dotsc,y_{\ell_{2}}\}
representing the bits of
x
and
y
. Since each bit is paired up with exactly one other bit, we call this a
matching
. For example, the four diagrams in
fig.

6
produce the following matchings:

(a), (b)

\displaystyle\{(x_{1},y_{3}),(x_{2},y_{1}),(x_{3},y_{2})\},

(c)

\displaystyle\{(x_{1},y_{2}),(x_{2},x_{3}),(y_{1},y_{3})\},

(d)

\displaystyle\{(x_{1},y_{3}),(x_{2},y_{1}),(x_{3},y_{2}),(x_{4},x_{5})\}.

(a)
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}
(b)
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}
(c)
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}
(d)
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
x_{4}
x_{5}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}

Figure 6:
Four different instances of
eq.

B.22
represented in graphical notation. Different permutations
\pi
result in different matchings among the bits of
x
and
y
induced by singlet chains. Each singlet
|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle=(|{01}\rangle-|{10}\rangle)/\sqrt{2}
is denoted by an arrow pointing from its first qubit to the second, see
eq.

B.23
. The left two matchings (a) and (b) are proper since the bits of
x
are bijectively matched with the bits of
y
, while the right two matchings (c) and (d) are improper, since two bits of
x
or
y
are matched.

We call a matching
proper
if each
x_{i}
is matched to some
y_{j}
(
e.g.
, matchings (a) and (b)); otherwise we call it
improper
(
e.g.
, matchings (c) and (d)). Note that an improper matching always pairs up two bits of
x
or two bits of
y
. In particular, if the strings
x
and
y
have different lengths, such as in case (d), the matching will always be improper.

To evaluate
eq.

B.22
, we will use the following restatement of
A.4
in terms of the normalized singlet state
|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle=|{S}\rangle/\sqrt{2}
.

Corollary B.3
.

For any
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\big\lparen I\otimes\langle{\Psi^{-}}|^{\otimes k}\big\rparen\big\lparen|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle^{\otimes k}\otimes I\big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k}}(-1)^{k}I,

(B.24)

\displaystyle\big\lparen I\otimes\langle{\Psi^{-}}|^{\otimes k}\otimes I\big\rparen\big\lparen|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle^{\otimes k+1}\big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k}}(-1)^{k}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle,

(B.25)

\displaystyle\langle{\Psi^{-}}|\SWAP\big\lparen I\otimes\langle{\Psi^{-}}|^{\otimes k}\otimes I\big\rparen\big\lparen|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle^{\otimes k+1}\big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k}}(-1)^{k+1}.

(B.26)

Moreover, if
j
singlets are in the opposite direction, i.e.,
|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle
is replaced by
\SWAP|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle=-|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle
, the final value of each expression picks up an extra factor of
(-1)^{j}
.

The following result shows that certain matrix entries of
P(\pi)
in the Schur basis vanish whenever the matching resulting from the corresponding two basis vectors is improper.

Proposition B.4
.

Let
n\geq 1
and fix any partitions
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\mathbin{\vdash}n
and
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\mathbin{\vdash}n
, and a permutation
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}
. If the diagram corresponding to

\langle{(\lambda,w,0)}|P(\pi)|{(\mu,v,0)}\rangle

(B.27)

yields an improper matching, the expression vanishes irrespective of the Hamming weights
w\in[m_{\lambda}]
and
v\in[m_{\mu}]
. In particular, it vanishes whenever
\lambda\neq\mu
.

Proof.

If
\lambda\neq\mu
then
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\neq\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}
, so there will be more bits on one side of the diagram than the other (
e.g.
, case (d) in
fig.

6
), so a proper matching between them is impossible.

To establish the main claim, we first write

\lambda=(r_{1}+\ell_{1},r_{1})
and

\mu=(r_{2}+\ell_{2},r_{2})

for some integers
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0
such that

\ell_{1}+2r_{1}=\ell_{2}+2r_{2}=n

(see
fig.

4
).
Next, recall from
eqs.

B.1
and
B.2
that

\langle{(\lambda,w,0)}|=\binom{\ell_{1}}{w}^{-1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{1}}\\
\lvert x\rvert=w\end{subarray}}\langle{x}|\otimes\langle{\Psi^{-}}|^{\otimes r_{1}},

(B.28)

and similarly for
|{(\mu,v,0)}\rangle
.
Hence, we can expand (
B.27
) as follows:

\binom{\ell_{1}}{w}^{-1/2}\binom{\ell_{2}}{v}^{-1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{1}}\\
\lvert x\rvert=w\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}}\\
\lvert y\rvert=v\end{subarray}}\langle{x}|\otimes\langle{\Psi^{-}}|^{\otimes r_{1}}\cdot P(\pi)\cdot|{y}\rangle\otimes|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle^{\otimes r_{2}}.

(B.29)

Each term in this expansion can be represented by one of the diagrams discussed before.
Since the wiring of the diagram depends only on
\pi
and the number of singlets
r_{1},r_{2}
, it is in fact the same diagram for all terms, except with different values of the bit strings
x
and
y
substituted in the gray circles (see examples in
fig.

6
).
Note that this expression can be factorized over all paths and cycles in the diagram.
Hence, to show that it vanishes, it suffices to find a single path with a vanishing contribution.

By assumption, the diagram corresponding to
eq.

B.29
yields an improper matching, meaning that two bits on the same side, say
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, are matched with each other. Hence, the number of singlets (or arrows) on the path between them is odd. According to
eq.

B.25
in
B.3
, such path is equivalent to a single singlet. More specifically,
eq.

B.25
allows us to replace a path of
2k+1
singlets by a contracted path consisting only of
(-1)^{k+j}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle/2^{k}
, where
j
is the number of singlets on the original path that point in the direction opposite to the singlet on the contracted path.
15
15

15

This can also be seen by iteratively contracting two singlets at a time using the identities displayed in
fig.

7
.

(B.30)

Since our goal is to show that the expression (
B.29
) vanishes, we can ignore the sign and the normalization, and simply treat the contracted path as
|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle
.
Hence, it suffices to analyze only the case when the path from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
consists of a single singlet.
In this case, the relevant part of the diagram looks as follows:

(B.31)

which means that we can pull out from (
B.29
) a factor of

\sum_{h\in H}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{0,1\}^{2}\\
|x|=h\end{subarray}}\langle{x_{1},x_{2}}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle,

(B.32)

where the range of
h
is some
16
16

16

More specifically,

H:=\{\max\{2-(\ell_{1}-w),0\},\dotsc,\min\{w,2\}\}
.
subset
H\subseteq\{0,1,2\}
that depends on the length
\ell_{1}
of
x
and its Hamming weight
w
(or the analogous parameters
\ell_{2}
and
v
if the permutation
\pi
matched up two bits of
y
instead of
x
).
Note that (
B.32
) vanishes for any

H\subseteq\{0,1,2\}
because

\displaystyle\langle{0,0}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle{0,1}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle+\langle{1,0}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle{1,1}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

(B.33)

Since the pre-factor (
B.32
) vanishes, then so does the whole expression (
B.29
).
Moreover, this phenomenon is independent of the values of
w
and
v
.
∎

While we will not need this, a more elaborate version of the above argument can be used to show a more general result that subsumes
B.2
and
B.4
, and determines the top left entry
\langle{0}|P_{\lambda}(\pi)|{0}\rangle
of the matrix
P_{\lambda}(\pi)
implicitly defined in
eq.

B.4
.
Note that the remaining entries of
P_{\lambda}(\pi)
cannot be determined in principle unless one makes a specific choice of the coefficients
\alpha_{\pi}^{\lambda,i}
in
eq.

B.3
that orthogonalize the states
|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle
.

Lemma B.5
.

Let
n\geq 1
and fix any partitions
\lambda,\mu\mathbin{\vdash}n
, Hamming weights
w\in[m_{\lambda}]
and
v\in[m_{\mu}]
, and a permutation
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}
. Then

\langle{(\lambda,w,0)}|P(\pi)|{(\mu,v,0)}\rangle=\delta_{\lambda\mu}\delta_{wv}\langle{0}|P_{\lambda}(\pi)|{0}\rangle,

(B.34)

where

\langle{0}|P_{\lambda}(\pi)|{0}\rangle=\begin{cases}\mathrm{sign}(\gamma)2^{\mathrm{loops}(\gamma)-\lambda_{2}}&\text{if $\gamma$ induces a proper matching},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(B.35)

Here
\gamma
denotes the diagram depicting
\langle{(\lambda,w,0)}|P(\pi)|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle
which depends only on
\pi
and
\lambda
(see
fig.

6
for some examples and the description below
eq.

B.22
for definition),

\mathrm{sign}(\gamma)\in\{+1,-1\}
is the sign of
\gamma
,
and
\mathrm{loops}(\gamma)
is the number of loops in
\gamma

(see proof for more details).

Proof.

We can proceed the same way as in the proof of
B.4
, except that now we want to obtain an explicit formula for (
B.29
).
As before, we observe that the matching induced by (
B.29
) is not proper unless
\lambda=\mu
, which gives us the first Kronecker delta function
\delta_{\lambda\mu}
.
If
\lambda=\mu
, we can use the same argument as in
lemma

B.1
to obtain the second Kronecker delta function
\delta_{wv}
.
Namely, we notice from
eq.

B.1
that the standard basis expansions of
\langle{(\lambda,w,0)}|
and
|{(\mu,v,0)}\rangle
contain only states of Hamming weights
w+\lambda_{2}
and
v+\mu_{2}
, respectively.
Since the permutation
P(\pi)
preserves Hamming weight,
eq.

B.29
vanishes unless
w=v
.

From now on we assume that
\lambda=\mu
and
w=v
, and our goal is to derive a formula for
\langle{(\lambda,w,0)}|P(\pi)|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle
.
Note that in this case
eq.

B.29
simplifies to

\langle{(\lambda,w,0)}|P(\pi)|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle=\binom{\ell}{w}^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\\
\lvert x\rvert=\lvert y\rvert=w\end{subarray}}\langle{x}|\otimes\langle{\Psi^{-}}|^{\otimes\lambda_{2}}\cdot P(\pi)\cdot|{y}\rangle\otimes|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle^{\otimes\lambda_{2}}

(B.36)

where
\ell:=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
.
There are two cases, depending on the diagram
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
that depicts the terms on the right-hand side of
eq.

B.36
. If the matching induced by
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
is improper,
eq.

B.36
vanishes independently of
w
by the same argument as in
B.4
. Otherwise, the bits of
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
are bijectively matched up by
\ell
chains of singlets, where each chain contains an even number of singlets (see examples (a) and (b) in
fig.

6
).
In addition,
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
may also contain some loops with an even number of singlets in each of them.

We can completely contract all singlet chains and loops in
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
either by removing two singlets at a time by using graphical notation (see
fig.

7
) or by applying
B.3
(use
eq.

B.24
to contract singlet chains between bits
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
, and
eq.

B.26
to contract singlet loops).
Note that we can uniformly use the same contraction procedure for all terms in
eq.

B.36
since they correspond to the same diagram
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
.

The contracted diagram has no more singlets but consists only of the bits of
x
and
y
with direct connections between them.
Since the original diagram yielded a proper matching, the contracted diagram encodes a Kronecker delta function between
x
and a permutation of
y
, which establishes a bijection between the values of
x
and
y
.
Since non-matching pairs of
x
and
y
drop out, we can discard one of the sums in
eq.

B.36
.
Moreover, the remaining terms are all equal since they depend only on the diagram
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
, so we can forget about the second sum as well and cancel out the binomial coefficient
\binom{\ell}{w}
.
This means that the right-hand side of
eq.

B.36
does not depend on
w
, and it can be computed by evaluating a single non-vanishing term whose diagram is
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
.
In particular, it is appropriate to denote the resulting value by
\langle{0}|P_{\lambda}(\pi)|{0}\rangle
, as in
eq.

B.34
, since it depends only on
\pi
and
\lambda
.
We will now separately determine the absolute value and the sign of
\langle{0}|P_{\lambda}(\pi)|{0}\rangle
as a function of the diagram
\gamma=\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
.

=
=
=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}SS
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}I
=
=
=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}SS^{\mathsf{T}}
\displaystyle\phantom{+}\frac{1}{2}I
=
=
=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}S^{\mathsf{T}}S
\displaystyle\phantom{+}\frac{1}{2}I
=
=
=
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}S^{\mathsf{T}}S^{\mathsf{T}}
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}I

Figure 7:
Contraction of two consecutive singlets
|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle=|{S}\rangle/\sqrt{2}
via
eq.

B.24
. The result has
negative
sign if both singlets point in the same direction and positive sign otherwise.

Since each term of
eq.

B.36
contains
2\lambda_{2}
singlets, with a normalization of
2^{-1/2}
each, we need to introduce a factor of
2^{-\lambda_{2}}
to compensate for their removal during contraction.
Moreover, each contracted loop contributes an additional factor of
\Tr I_{2}=2
, so the final result has an absolute value of
\lvert\langle{0}|P_{\lambda}(\pi)|{0}\rangle\rvert=2^{\mathrm{loops}(\gamma)-\lambda_{2}}
.
The sign of
\langle{0}|P_{\lambda}(\pi)|{0}\rangle
is determined by accounting for the singlet arrows in
\gamma
.
According to
B.3
, contracting a path or a loop of
2k
singlets that all point in the same direction produces the sign
(-1)^{k}
.
The overall sign of
\gamma
, which we denoted by
\mathrm{sign}(\gamma)
, is then determined by taking the product of individual signs over all paths and loops.
Since
\SWAP|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle=-|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle
, the sign must be inverted whenever the direction of any singlet is inverted (this rule is consistent since we are dealing with the case when each path and loop has an even number of singlets).
∎

B.2
Orthonormality

In this section, we show that the vectors
|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle
form an orthonormal basis of the
n
-qubit space
\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}}
.
First, in
B.6
we establish orthonormality only for subsets indexed by
i
, and then in
lemma

B.7
we establish it for the full set.

Proposition B.6
.

Let
n\geq 1
and fix any partition
\lambda\mathbin{\vdash}n
and Hamming weight
w\in[m_{\lambda}]
. Then, for any
i,j\in[d_{\lambda}]
,

\langle{(\lambda,w,i)}|{(\lambda,w,j)}\rangle=\delta_{ij}.

(B.37)

Proof.

Recall from
eq.

B.3
that

|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}}\alpha_{\pi}^{\lambda,i}P(\pi)|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle

(B.38)

where the coefficients
\alpha_{\pi}^{\lambda,i}\in\mathbb{R}
are chosen so that
\langle{(\lambda,0,i)}|{(\lambda,0,j)}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
, see
eq.

3.15
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\langle{(\lambda,w,i)}|{(\lambda,w,j)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{(\lambda,w,0)}|\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}}\alpha_{\pi}^{\lambda,i}P(\pi)^{\dagger}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{S}_{n}}\alpha_{\sigma}^{\lambda,j}P(\sigma)|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle

(B.39)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi,\sigma\in\mathrm{S}_{n}}\alpha_{\pi}^{\lambda,i}\alpha_{\sigma}^{\lambda,j}\langle{(\lambda,w,0)}|P(\pi^{-1}\sigma)|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle.

(B.40)

Using
B.2
, we can replace
w
by
0
and perform the same calculation in reverse. This produces
\langle{(\lambda,0,i)}|{(\lambda,0,j)}\rangle
, which is equal to the desired value
\delta_{ij}
by
eq.

3.15
.
∎

Orthonormality of the full set of
|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle
follows by combining
lemmas

B.1
and
B.6
.

Lemma B.7
.

For any
n\geq 1
, partitions
\lambda,\mu\mathbin{\vdash}n
, Hamming weights
w\in[m_{\lambda}]
and
v\in[m_{\mu}]
, and indices
i\in[d_{\lambda}]
and
j\in[d_{\mu}]
,

\langle{(\lambda,w,i)}|{(\mu,v,j)}\rangle=\delta_{\lambda\mu}\delta_{wv}\delta_{ij}.

(B.41)

Proof.

If
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}
is the trivial permutation then
P(\pi)=I_{2^{n}}
. We already know from
lemma

B.1
that, for any
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}
,

\langle{(\lambda,w,i)}|P(\pi)|{(\mu,v,j)}\rangle=\delta_{\lambda\mu}\delta_{wv}\langle{i}|P_{\lambda}(\pi)|{j}\rangle,

(B.42)

for some operator
P_{\lambda}(\pi)\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{\lambda}})
. This expression vanishes when
\lambda\neq\mu
or
w\neq v
, so it only remains to understand the case when
\lambda=\mu
and
w=v
. In this case,

\langle{(\lambda,w,i)}|{(\lambda,w,j)}\rangle=\delta_{ij}

(B.43)

according to
B.6
.
∎

B.3
Unitary action

This section considers the action of
M^{\otimes n}
on the vectors
|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle
for any
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
As a special case, this also includes the action of
U^{\otimes n}
where
U\in\mathrm{U}(2)
is unitary.

Lemma B.8
.

For any
n\geq 1
, partition
\lambda\mathbin{\vdash}n
, and index
i\in[d_{\lambda}]
,
the corresponding subspace

\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda,i}:=\spn\big\{|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle:w\in[m_{\lambda}]\big\}

is invariant under
M^{\otimes n}
for all
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Proof.

For any
\ell\geq 0
,
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}
, and
w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
,

P(\pi)M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle=M^{\otimes\ell}P(\pi)|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle=M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle,

(B.44)

so the vector
M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle
is fixed by all qubit permutations. Hence, the
\ell
-qubit symmetric space,
\spn\{|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle:w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}\}
, is invariant under
M^{\otimes\ell}
and we can write

M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle=\sum_{v\in[m_{\lambda}]}\beta^{\prime}_{v}|{s_{\ell}(v)}\rangle,

(B.45)

for some
\beta^{\prime}_{v}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Combining the above and the fact that

(M\otimes M)|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle=\det(M)|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle

together with the definition of
|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle
from
eq.

3.11
, we get

\displaystyle M^{\otimes n}|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle=M^{\otimes(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})}|{s_{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}(w)}\rangle\otimes\left\lparen\det(M)|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle\right\rparen^{\otimes\lambda_{2}}=\sum_{v\in[m_{\lambda}]}\beta_{v}|{(\lambda,v,0)}\rangle,

(B.46)

where
\beta_{v}:=\beta^{\prime}_{v}\lparen\det(M)\rparen^{\lambda_{2}}
.
Using the definition of
|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle
from
eq.

B.3
,

\displaystyle M^{\otimes n}|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}}\alpha_{\pi}^{\lambda,i}P(\pi)\Big(M^{\otimes n}|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle\Big)

(B.47)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}}\alpha_{\pi}^{\lambda,i}P(\pi)\sum_{v\in[m_{\lambda}]}\beta_{v}|{(\lambda,v,0)}\rangle

(B.48)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in[m_{\lambda}]}\beta_{v}\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}}\alpha_{\pi}^{\lambda,i}P(\pi)|{(\lambda,v,0)}\rangle\right)

(B.49)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in[m_{\lambda}]}\beta_{v}|{(\lambda,v,i)}\rangle,

(B.50)

where the final state lies in the subspace
\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda,i}
as desired.
∎

Having established that the subspaces spanned by
|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle
with
w\in[m_{\lambda}]
are invariant under the action of
M^{\otimes n}
, we would like to derive explicit matrix entries of this action.
We will show that they coincide with
Q_{\lambda}(M)
, where
Q_{\lambda}
is the representation of
\mathrm{U}(2)
defined in
section

C.5
.
This stems from the fact that the vectors
|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle
are related to
|{(\lambda,w,0)}\rangle
, which can in turn be expressed in terms of the symmetric states
|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle
and singlets
|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle
.
The result the follows from
lemma

D.2
in
appendix

D
which is an analogous statement for the symmetric states
|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle
.

Lemma B.9
.

For any
n\geq 1
, partitions
\lambda,\mu\mathbin{\vdash}n
, Hamming weights
w\in[m_{\lambda}]
and
v\in[m_{\mu}]
, indices
i\in[d_{\lambda}]
and
j\in[d_{\mu}]
, and matrix
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

\displaystyle M^{\otimes n}|{(\mu,v,j)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=0}^{m_{\mu}}Q_{\mu}(M)_{wv}|{(\mu,w,j)}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle{(\lambda,w,i)}|M^{\otimes n}|{(\mu,v,j)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{\lambda\mu}\delta_{ij}Q_{\mu}(M)_{wv},

(B.51)

where

m_{\mu}:=\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}+1
and
Q_{\mu}:\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\to\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{\mu}})
is the representation defined in
eq.

C.39
.

Proof.

By
lemma

B.7
, the vectors
|{(\lambda,w,i)}\rangle
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}}
, so the second identity follows by multiplying both sides of the first identity by
\langle{(\lambda,w,i)}|
.

To prove the first identity, note from
eq.

B.3
that

M^{\otimes n}|{(\mu,v,j)}\rangle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{n}}\alpha_{\pi}^{\mu,j}P(\pi)M^{\otimes n}|{(\mu,v,0)}\rangle.

(B.52)

Recall from
eq.

B.1
that

|{(\mu,v,0)}\rangle:=|{s_{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}(v)}\rangle\otimes|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle^{\otimes\mu_{2}}
.
Since the singlet state satisfies

(M\otimes M)|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle=\det(M)|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle
,

lemma

D.2
implies that

\displaystyle M^{\otimes n}|{(\mu,v,0)}\rangle

\displaystyle=M^{\otimes\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}|{s_{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}(v)}\rangle\otimes M^{\otimes 2\mu_{2}}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle^{\otimes\mu_{2}}

(B.53)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=0}^{m_{\mu}}t^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}_{wv}(M)\,|{s_{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}(w)}\rangle\otimes(\det M)^{\mu_{2}}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle^{\otimes\mu_{2}}

(B.54)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=0}^{m_{\mu}}Q_{\mu}(M)_{wv}|{(\mu,w,0)}\rangle,

(B.55)

where we used the definition of
Q_{\mu}
from
eq.

C.39
.
The result follows by substituting this in
eq.

B.52
and exchanging the two sums.
∎

Appendix C
Representations of
2\times 2
matrices

In this appendix, we briefly summarize the representation theory of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
and
\mathrm{U}(2)
.
While representations of
\mathrm{U}(2)
are defined as homomorphisms of the form
R:\mathrm{U}(2)\to\mathrm{U}(d)
for some
d\geq 1
, the entries of the represented matrix
R(U)
are actually polynomials (or more generally rational functions) in the entries of the original matrix
U
.
We take the perspective of
[
Lou08
]
and treat the matrix
U
as if having symbolic entries
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
so that we can think of
R(U)
as a matrix consisting of polynomials.
This has the advantage that we can represent not just unitary matrices but any
2\times 2
matrix, whether numeric or symbolic.
In particular, we can represent the set
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
of all
2\times 2
complex matrices, viewed as a monoid under matrix multiplication
[
Bae22
]
.

C.1
Construction

Let us recap an explicit construction of all irreducible representations of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
.
A concise derivation of this construction is given in
[
Koo85
]
.
For more details, see
[
Vil78
, p. 108]
,
[
VK91
, p. 276]
,
[
Waw84
, p. 279]
, or
[
Žel73
, p. 90]
.

For any function
\psi:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
and matrix
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, define the corresponding transformed function
\psi_{M}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
via

\psi_{M}(v):=\psi(vM),\quad\forall v\in\mathbb{C}^{2},

(C.1)

where
v
is treated as a row vector. More specifically, if
v=\begin{pmatrix}x&y\end{pmatrix}
and
M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
then

\psi_{M}(x,y)=\psi(ax+cy,bx+dy).

(C.2)

Let us fix an integer
17
17

17

In physics, it is common to use the half-integer
\ell/2
instead of
\ell
.

\ell\geq 0
and construct an
(\ell+1)
-dimensional representation of
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
that acts on
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}:=\spn\{x^{\ell},x^{\ell-1}y,\dotsc,xy^{\ell-1},y^{\ell}\}
, the complex linear space of homogeneous degree-
\ell
bivariate polynomials. We can treat any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
as a function
\psi:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
by evaluating it at a given point
x,y\in\mathbb{C}
. For each
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, define a map
T^{\ell}(M):\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\to\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
that applies
M
to the arguments of
\psi
:

T^{\ell}(M):\psi\mapsto\psi_{M},\quad\forall\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}.

(C.3)

Note from
eq.

C.2
that
\psi_{M}
is also homogeneous and of degree
\ell
.
Moreover, note from
eq.

C.1
that
(\psi_{M_{1}})_{M_{2}}=\psi_{M_{1}M_{2}}
, since
(vM_{1})M_{2}=v(M_{1}M_{2})
for all
v\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
This implies that
T^{\ell}(M_{1}M_{2})=T^{\ell}(M_{1})T^{\ell}(M_{2})
, meaning that
T^{\ell}
is a representation of
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
on the
(\ell+1)
-dimensional space
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
, where we view
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
as a monoid
18
18

18

Note that the map
T^{\ell}
is
not
linear, so it does
not
constitute a representation of
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
as an algebra.
under matrix multiplication
[
Bae22
]
.

To compute the matrix entries of
T^{\ell}(M)
in
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
, we introduce the following basis consisting of normalized degree-
\ell
monomials:

\psi^{\ell}_{k}(x,y):=\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}x^{\ell-k}y^{k}

(C.4)

where
k\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
.
In this basis,

T^{\ell}(M)\psi^{\ell}_{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}t^{\ell}_{jk}(M)\psi^{\ell}_{j},

(C.5)

where
t^{\ell}_{jk}(M)
are the matrix entries of
T^{\ell}(M)
.
Substituting the definition of
T^{\ell}(M)
from
eqs.

C.3
and
C.2
, and
\psi^{\ell}_{k}
from
eq.

C.4
,

\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}(ax+cy)^{\ell-k}(bx+dy)^{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}t^{\ell}_{jk}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\right]\binom{\ell}{j}^{1/2}x^{\ell-j}y^{j}.

(C.6)

To obtain a formula for
t^{\ell}_{jk}
, we need to compare the coefficients at
x^{\ell-j}y^{j}
on both sides.

Expanding the left-hand side of
eq.

C.6
yields

\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{\ell-k}\binom{\ell-k}{r}(ax)^{r}(cy)^{\ell-k-r}\sum_{s=0}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(bx)^{s}(dy)^{k-s}.

(C.7)

To compare this with the right-hand side of
eq.

C.6
, we need to obtain the coefficient at
x^{\ell-j}y^{j}
for
j\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
.
If we pick
s:=\ell-j-r
in the second sum, we can guarantee that
s\geq 0
and
s\leq k
by restricting
r
so that
\ell-j\geq r
and
\ell-j-k\leq r
, respectively.
This produces

\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}\sum_{r=\max\lparen\ell-j-k,0\rparen}^{\min\lparen\ell-j,\ell-k\rparen}\binom{\ell-k}{r}a^{r}c^{\ell-k-r}\binom{k}{\ell-j-r}b^{\ell-j-r}d^{j+k+r-\ell}.

(C.8)

Comparing this with the coefficient at
x^{\ell-j}y^{j}
on the right-hand side of
eq.

C.6
, we get the following formula for the matrix entries of
T^{\ell}(M)
:

\displaystyle t^{\ell}_{jk}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\right]:=\sqrt{j!(\ell-j)!k!(\ell-k)!}\sum_{r=\max\lparen\ell-j-k,0\rparen}^{\min\lparen\ell-j,\ell-k\rparen}\frac{a^{r}b^{\ell-j-r}c^{\ell-k-r}d^{j+k+r-\ell}}{r!(\ell-j-r)!(\ell-k-r)!(j+k+r-\ell)!},

(C.9)

for any integer
\ell\geq 0
and
j,k\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
.
In physics literature,
T^{\ell}(M)
is known as
Wigner
D
-matrix
since it was first derived by Wigner (see eq. (15.21) on p. 164 of
[
Wig59
]
).
The basis in which these representations are written is known as the
Gelfand–Tsetlin basis
, see
[
GT88
]
,
[
Lou08
, Section 11.3]
,
[
VK13
, Chapter 18]
, or
[
Žel73
, Section 67]
.

As advocated in
[
Lou08
]
, it is useful to think of
M
as a symbolic matrix and of
T^{\ell}(M)
as a collection of homogeneous polynomials in the entries of
M
. Since
T^{\ell}(M_{1}M_{2})=T^{\ell}(M_{1})T^{\ell}(M_{2})
even for
symbolic

2\times 2
matrices
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, we can view
T^{\ell}
as a representation of any
2\times 2
matrix group, such as
\mathrm{SU}(2)
,
\mathrm{U}(2)
,
\mathrm{SL}(2)
,
\mathrm{GL}(2)
, or even the full set of all
2\times 2
complex matrices
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, viewed as a monoid under matrix multiplication
[
Bae22
]
.

The first few values of
\ell
yield the following expressions for
T^{\ell}(M)
:

\displaystyle T^{0}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1\end{pmatrix},\quad T^{1}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\right]=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix},\quad T^{2}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\right]=\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}&\sqrt{2}ab&b^{2}\\
\sqrt{2}ac&ad+bc&\sqrt{2}bd\\
c^{2}&\sqrt{2}cd&d^{2}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle T^{3}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a^{3}&\sqrt{3}a^{2}b&\sqrt{3}ab^{2}&b^{3}\\
\sqrt{3}a^{2}c&a(ad+2bc)&b(2ad+bc)&\sqrt{3}b^{2}d\\
\sqrt{3}ac^{2}&c(2ad+bc)&d(ad+2bc)&\sqrt{3}bd^{2}\\
c^{3}&\sqrt{3}c^{2}d&\sqrt{3}cd^{2}&d^{3}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle T^{4}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a^{4}&2a^{3}b&\sqrt{6}a^{2}b^{2}&2ab^{3}&b^{4}\\
2a^{3}c&a^{2}(ad+3bc)&\sqrt{6}ab(ad+bc)&b^{2}(3ad+bc)&2b^{3}d\\
\sqrt{6}a^{2}c^{2}&\sqrt{6}ac(ad+bc)&a^{2}d^{2}+4abcd+b^{2}c^{2}&\sqrt{6}bd(ad+bc)&\sqrt{6}b^{2}d^{2}\\
2ac^{3}&c^{2}(3ad+bc)&\sqrt{6}cd(ad+bc)&d^{2}(ad+3bc)&2bd^{3}\\
c^{4}&2c^{3}d&\sqrt{6}c^{2}d^{2}&2cd^{3}&d^{4}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Since
T^{1}(M)=M
, this is also known as the
defining representation
.

C.2
Properties

By construction, the map
T^{\ell}
defined by
eq.

C.9
is a representation of
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, meaning that
T^{\ell}(M_{1}M_{2})=T^{\ell}(M_{1})T^{\ell}(M_{2})
for all
M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
Let us denote its dimension by

m_{\ell}:=\ell+1

(C.10)

and briefly discuss several of its properties.

First, notice from
eq.

C.9
that all matrix entries of
T^{\ell}(M)
are homogeneous degree-
\ell
polynomials in the entries of
M
.
Hence,
T^{\ell}(tM)=t^{\ell}T(M)
, for any
t\in\mathbb{C}
.

Next, if we assume that
M
is diagonal, say
M=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&d\end{pmatrix}
, then
\psi_{M}(x,y)=\psi(ax,dy)
by
eq.

C.2
.
According to
eq.

C.4
,
\psi^{\ell}_{k}(ax,dy)=a^{\ell-k}d^{k}\psi^{\ell}_{k}(x,y)
, so

T^{\ell}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&d\end{pmatrix}\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}a^{\ell-k}d^{k}|{k}\rangle\langle{k}|

(C.11)

is diagonal as well.
In the special cases when
a=d=1
or when
a=1
and
d=0
we get

\displaystyle T^{\ell}(I_{2})

\displaystyle=I_{m_{\ell}},

\displaystyle T^{\ell}\big\lparen|{0}\rangle\langle{0}|_{2}\big\rparen

\displaystyle=|{0}\rangle\langle{0}|_{m_{\ell}},

(C.12)

respectively, where the subscripts indicate dimensions and we use the convention that
0^{0}=1
.

Next, note that
eq.

C.9
is invariant under simultaneously exchanging
j\leftrightarrow k
and
b\leftrightarrow c
:

t^{\ell}_{jk}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\right]=t^{\ell}_{kj}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&c\\
b&d\end{pmatrix}\right],

(C.13)

which is ultimately thanks to our choice of normalization in
eq.

C.4
.
This property is equivalent to
T^{\ell}(M^{\mathsf{T}})=T^{\ell}(M)^{\mathsf{T}}
.
Since
T^{\ell}(\overline{M})=\overline{T^{\ell}(M)}
, we also get
T^{\ell}(M^{\dagger})=T^{\ell}(M)^{\dagger}
.
By combining these observations we conclude that if
M
is unitary,
i.e.
,
MM^{\dagger}=I_{2}
, then
T^{\ell}(M)T^{\ell}(M)^{\dagger}=T^{\ell}(MM^{\dagger})=T^{\ell}(I_{2})=I_{m_{\ell}}
, meaning that
T^{\ell}(M)
is also unitary.
In other words, not only is
T^{\ell}
a representation of
\mathrm{U}(2)
and
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, it is also a
unitary
representation.

Combining unitarity with
eq.

C.11
we conclude that if
M
has singular values
\{a,d\}
,
i.e.
,
M=U\diag(a,d)V^{\dagger}
for some unitaries
U,V\in\mathrm{U}(2)
, then

T^{\ell}(M)=T^{\ell}(U)\diag\lparen a^{\ell-k}d^{k}:k\in[m_{\ell}]\rparen T^{\ell}(V^{\dagger})

(C.14)

has singular values
\{a^{\ell-k}d^{k}:k\in[m_{\ell}]\}
.
In particular,
T^{\ell}(|{\psi}\rangle\langle{\varphi}|)
is a rank-
1
matrix for any
|{\psi}\rangle,|{\varphi}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

The following proposition provides a simple formula for the image of a pure state
|{\psi}\rangle\langle{\psi}|
under
T^{\ell}
.
In physics, the resulting state
|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle\langle{\psi^{\ell}}|
is known as
coherent spin state

[
Rad71
,
ACGT72
]
.

Proposition C.1

(Image of a pure state)
.

Let
|{\psi}\rangle=\begin{pmatrix}a\\
c\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
Then, for any
\ell\geq 0
,

T^{\ell}\big\lparen|{\psi}\rangle\langle{\psi}|\big\rparen=|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle\langle{\psi^{\ell}}|,\qquad\text{where}\qquad|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle:=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\psi^{\ell}_{k}(a,c)|{k}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{m_{\ell}}

(C.15)

and
\psi^{\ell}_{k}
is the function from
eq.

C.4
.
Moreover,
|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle
is a unit vector if and only if
|{\psi}\rangle
is.

Proof.

Let
M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
so that
M|{0}\rangle=\begin{pmatrix}a\\
c\end{pmatrix}=|{\psi}\rangle
. Then using
eq.

C.12
we get

T^{\ell}\big\lparen|{\psi}\rangle\langle{\psi}|\big\rparen=T^{\ell}\big\lparen M|{0}\rangle\langle{0}|M^{\dagger}\big\rparen=T^{\ell}(M)|{0}\rangle\langle{0}|_{m_{\ell}}T^{\ell}(M)^{\dagger},

(C.16)

so it only remains to show that
T^{\ell}(M)|{0}\rangle_{m_{\ell}}=|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle_{m_{\ell}}
.
According to the definition of
T^{\ell}
from
eqs.

C.2
and
C.3
,
T^{\ell}(M)
acts on the monomial
\psi^{\ell}_{0}(x,y)
as follows:

\displaystyle T^{\ell}(M)\psi^{\ell}_{0}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\psi^{\ell}_{0}(ax+cy,bx+dy)

(C.17)

\displaystyle=(ax+cy)^{\ell}

(C.18)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{k}(ax)^{\ell-k}(cy)^{k}

(C.19)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\psi^{\ell}_{k}(a,c)\psi^{\ell}_{k}(x,y),

(C.20)

where we used the definition of
\psi^{\ell}_{k}(x,y)
from
eq.

C.4
several times.
This identity is equivalent to
T^{\ell}(M)|{0}\rangle_{m_{\ell}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\psi^{\ell}_{k}(a,c)|{k}\rangle_{m_{\ell}}=|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle
, which is what we had to show.
Finally, note that

\langle{\psi^{\ell}}|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{k}\lparen\lvert a\rvert^{2}\rparen^{\ell-k}\lparen\lvert c\rvert^{2}\rparen^{k}=\left\lparen\lvert a\rvert^{2}+\lvert c\rvert^{2}\right\rparen^{\ell}=\lparen\langle{\psi}|{\psi}\rangle\rparen^{\ell},

(C.21)

meaning that
|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle
is normalized if and only if
|{\psi}\rangle
is.
∎

C.3
Irreducibility

Since the representation
T^{\ell}
has dimension
m_{\ell}=\ell+1
, it is clear that the
T^{\ell}
are distinct for different values of
\ell
.
However, it is not obvious that they are irreducible,
i.e.
, cannot be expressed as a direct sum.
For the sake of completeness, we provide a proof of this standard fact below.
For more details, see Proposition 8.2.3 on p. 285 of
[
Waw84
]
or Proposition 5.1 on p. 85 of
[
BtD85
]
.

Lemma C.2
.

For any integer
\ell\geq 0
, the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
representation
T^{\ell}
defined by
eq.

C.9
is irreducible.

Proof.

Fix the value of
\ell
and consider the matrix algebra
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}
generated by
T^{\ell}(U)
as
U
ranges over
\mathrm{SU}(2)
,
i.e.
,

\mathscr{A}^{\ell}:=\langle T^{\ell}(U):U\in\mathrm{SU}(2)\rangle.

(C.22)

In particular,
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}
also includes
T^{\ell}(U)
for infinitesimally small rotations
U
.
To conclude that
T^{\ell}
is irreducible, it suffices to show that
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}
is the full matrix algebra
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{\ell}})
.
We will show that
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}=\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{\ell}})
by explicitly expressing each elementary matrix
|{j}\rangle\langle{k}|
where
j,k\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
using the elements of
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}
.

Letting
\omega:=\exp(\frac{2\pi i}{m_{\ell}})
, note from
eq.

C.11
that,
for any
j\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{\ell}}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}\omega^{-mj}T^{\ell}\left[\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\omega^{m}\end{pmatrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{\ell}}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}\omega^{-mj}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\omega^{mk}|{k}\rangle\langle{k}|

(C.23)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\left\lparen\frac{1}{m_{\ell}}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}\omega^{m(k-j)}\right\rparen|{k}\rangle\langle{k}|

(C.24)

\displaystyle=|{j}\rangle\langle{j}|,

(C.25)

which constitutes all diagonal elements of
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{\ell}})
.

An explicit construction of off-diagonal elements of
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{\ell}})
is more involved (indeed, diagonal elements are a special case of this).
Let
X:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
and
Y:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}
denote the Pauli matrices and let

\displaystyle R_{X}(t)

\displaystyle:=\exp\left\lparen-i\tfrac{t}{2}X\right\rparen=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\frac{t}{2}&-i\sin\frac{t}{2}\\
-i\sin\frac{t}{2}&\cos\frac{t}{2}\end{pmatrix},

(C.26)

\displaystyle R_{Y}(t)

\displaystyle:=\exp\left\lparen-i\tfrac{t}{2}Y\right\rparen=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\frac{t}{2}&-\sin\frac{t}{2}\\
\sin\frac{t}{2}&\cos\frac{t}{2}\end{pmatrix}

(C.27)

denote the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
elements that rotate the Bloch sphere by angle
t
around the
x
and
y
axis, respectively.
Let us denote the infinitesimal versions of their
T^{\ell}
-representations by

\displaystyle X^{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{d}{dt}T^{\ell}(R_{X}(t)),

\displaystyle Y^{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{d}{dt}T^{\ell}(R_{Y}(t)).

(C.28)

Since
T^{\ell}(R_{X}(0))=T^{\ell}(I_{2})=I_{m_{\ell}}
, from the definition of a derivative we get that

X^{\ell}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{T^{\ell}(R_{X}(\varepsilon))-I_{m_{\ell}}}{\varepsilon},

(C.29)

and similarly for
Y^{\ell}
. This shows that
X^{\ell}
and
Y^{\ell}
are limits of elements that belong to the algebra
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}
generated by
T^{\ell}(U)
.
Since
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}
is a linear subspace of
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{\ell}})
, it is closed and thus
X^{\ell}
and
Y^{\ell}
themselves belong to
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}
.

We can use
X^{\ell}
and
Y^{\ell}
to generate more elements that belong to
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}
.
In particular, let

E_{\pm}^{\ell}:=iX^{\ell}\pm Y^{\ell}.

(C.30)

Using
eq.

C.9
, one can obtain the following explicit formula for their matrix entries:

E_{+}^{\ell}=(E_{-}^{\ell})^{\mathsf{T}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}\sqrt{(\ell-k)(k+1)}\;|{k+1}\rangle\langle{k}|,

(C.31)

cf.
, eq. (8.4.6) on p. 296 of
[
Waw84
]
.
In other words,
E_{\pm}^{\ell}|{k}\rangle\propto|{k\pm 1}\rangle
whenever
k\pm 1\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
, and
E_{\pm}^{\ell}|{k}\rangle=0
otherwise.
Hence, up to a constant factor,
E_{\pm}^{\ell}
corresponds to shifting
|{k}\rangle
by one in the corresponding direction.

For any
j\in\{1,\dotsc,\ell\}
, let us denote the
j
-th matrix power of
E_{\pm}^{\ell}
(with an appropriate normalization) by

S_{\pm}^{\ell}(j):=\sqrt{\frac{(\ell-j)!}{\ell!j!}}(E_{\pm}^{\ell})^{j}.

(C.32)

Since
E_{\pm}^{\ell}
are singular, we also set
S_{\pm}^{\ell}(0):=I_{m_{\ell}}
by hand.
Then
S_{\pm}^{\ell}(j)|{k}\rangle\propto|{k\pm j}\rangle
whenever
k\pm j\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
, and
S_{\pm}^{\ell}(j)|{k}\rangle=0
otherwise. In other words,
S_{\pm}^{\ell}(j)
performs a shift by
\pm j
, up to an overall constant factor.
The normalization in
eq.

C.32
is chosen so that, for any
j\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
,

\displaystyle S_{+}^{\ell}(j)|{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=|{j}\rangle,

\displaystyle S_{-}^{\ell}(j)|{\ell}\rangle

\displaystyle=|{\ell-j}\rangle,

(C.33)

i.e.
, no additional factors are incurred when shifting the first and the last vector, respectively.
By transposing the first formula and noting that
\smash[b]{S_{+}^{\ell}(j)}^{\mathsf{T}}=S_{-}^{\ell}(j)
, we also get

\langle{0}|S_{-}^{\ell}(j)=\langle{j}|.

(C.34)

Finally, note that the shifts by
\ell
have the following particularly simple matrix form:

S_{+}^{\ell}(\ell)=\smash[b]{S_{-}^{\ell}(\ell)}^{\mathsf{T}}=|{\ell}\rangle\langle{0}|.

(C.35)

By combining
eqs.

C.33
,
C.34
and
C.35
we can express any elementary matrix as follows:

S_{-}^{\ell}(\ell-i)S_{+}^{\ell}(\ell)S_{-}^{\ell}(j)=|{i}\rangle\langle{j}|,

(C.36)

where
i,j\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
are arbitrary.
This implies that
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}=\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{\ell}})
,
i.e.
,
\mathscr{A}^{\ell}
is the whole matrix algebra, and hence
T^{\ell}
is irreducible.
∎

C.4
Completeness

It turns out that every irreducible representation of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is equivalent to one of the representations
T^{\ell}
constructed in
section

C.1
.

Theorem C.3
.

Any irreducible representation of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is equivalent to
T^{\ell}
, for some integer
\ell\geq 0
.

For a proof, see Theorem 3 on p. 93 of
[
Žel73
]
,
Corollary 8.6.2 on p. 301 of
[
Waw84
]
,
or Proposition 5.3 on p. 86 of
[
BtD85
]
.
This constitutes a complete characterization of all irreducible representations of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
.

C.5
Representations of
\mathrm{U}(2)

Let us start with
1
-dimensional representations of
\mathrm{U}(2)
.
Any matrix in
\mathrm{U}(2)
can be written as
e^{i\varphi}U
, for some
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}
and
U\in\mathrm{SU}(2)
. Since
e^{i\varphi}\in\mathrm{U}(1)
, it is useful to first understand the irreducible representations of
\mathrm{U}(1)
. They correspond to non-zero rational functions
R:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
R(a)R(b)=R(ab)
, for all
a,b\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Any such function is of the form
R(a):=a^{r}
,
for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, which exhausts all irreducible representations of
\mathrm{U}(1)
.
If
D:\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\to\mathbb{C}
is a
1
-dimensional representation of
\mathrm{U}(2)
then so is
R\circ D
.
In particular, the determinant is a
1
-dimensional representation since
\det(MN)=\det(M)\det(N)
. Composing it with
R
we get

D^{r}(M):=(\det M)^{r}

where
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. This exhausts all
1
-dimensional (and thus irreducible) representations of
\mathrm{U}(2)
.

Any representation can be entry-wise multiplied by a
1
-dimensional representation to obtain a new representation. In particular, we can multiply any of the representations
T^{\ell}
constructed in
section

C.1
by
D^{r}
to obtain

T^{r,\ell}(M):=(\det M)^{r}T^{\ell}(M).

(C.37)

For each
\ell\geq 0
, this exhausts all
(\ell+1)
-dimensional irreducible representations of
\mathrm{U}(2)
as we range
r
over
\mathbb{Z}
.
Consequently, any irreducible representation of
\mathrm{U}(2)
is of the form
T^{r,\ell}
, for some integers
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\geq 0

[
Ste87
,
Sta83
]
.
Note from
eq.

C.9
that all entries of
T^{r,\ell}(M)
are homogeneous polynomials (or rational functions when
r<0
) of degree
2r+\ell
in the entries of
M
.

It is useful to establish a correspondence between the parameters
r,\ell
of the representation
T^{r,\ell}
and partitions
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
defined in
eq.

3.4
. When
r\geq 0
, the two are related as follows:

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=(r+\ell,r).

(C.38)

Note that
\lambda
is a partition of
n=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=2r+\ell
, which is also the total number of boxes in the corresponding Young diagram in
fig.

4
.

Given
\lambda\mathbin{\vdash}n
, we can recover
r=\lambda_{2}
and
\ell=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
via
eq.

C.38
, so it is natural to associate to
\lambda
the representation
T^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}
. We denote this representation by

Q_{\lambda}(M):=(\det M)^{\lambda_{2}}T^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}(M),

(C.39)

where the entries of
T^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}(M)
are given in
eq.

C.9
. These are precisely the representations that appear in the decomposition of
M^{\otimes n}
in Schur basis, see
eq.

3.6
in
section

3.1
.
Note that representations
T^{r,\ell}
with
r<0
do not appear in this decomposition since the corresponding
\lambda
is not a partition.

The matrix entries of
Q_{\lambda}(M)
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
n=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}
in the entries of
M
. In particular, they can be evaluated for any
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, so
Q_{\lambda}
is not only an irreducible representation of
\mathrm{U}(2)
but also of
\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
as a monoid under matrix multiplication
[
Bae22
]
.
Note from
eq.

C.39
that adding or removing a full column of boxes from the diagram of
\lambda
corresponds to multiplying or dividing the corresponding representation by
\det(M)
.
This observation extends also to
\mathrm{U}(d)
, for any
d\geq 1

[
Ste87
,
Sta83
]
.

C.6
Clebsch–Gordan transform

Recall from
section

C.2
that the matrix entries of
T^{\ell}(M)
are homogeneous degree-
\ell
polynomials in the entries of
M
.
Hence, the entries of
M\otimes T^{\ell}(M)
are also homogeneous but of degree
\ell+1
.
It turns out that they can be expressed as linear combinations of the entries of
T^{1,\ell-1}(M)
and
T^{0,\ell+1}(M)
, which are also polynomials of degree
\ell+1
.
The relationship between the two is expressed via Clebsch–Gordan transform, see
[
Boh93
, p. 165]
,
[
Vil78
, p. 174]
,
[
VK91
, p. 498]
,
[
Waw84
, p. 304]
.

For any integer
\ell\geq 0
, the
Clebsch–Gordan transform

C_{\ell}\in\mathrm{U}(2\ell+2)
is a map whose input and output spaces are interpreted as follows:

C_{\ell}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ell}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\ell+2}.

(C.40)

Its defining property is that, for all
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

C_{\ell}\big\lparen M\otimes T^{\ell}(M)\big\rparen C_{\ell}^{\dagger}=T^{1,\ell-1}(M)\oplus T^{0,\ell+1}(M).

(C.41)

Equivalently, in terms of
\lambda
and
Q_{\lambda}
,

C_{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\big\lparen M\otimes Q_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}(M)\big\rparen C_{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}^{\dagger}=Q_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}+1)}(M)\oplus Q_{(\lambda_{1}+1,\lambda_{2})}(M),

(C.42)

which holds for any
\lambda\mathbin{\vdash}n
.

Up to certain phases, such map
C_{\ell}
is essentially unique.
If we let

R^{\ell}(k):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell+1}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{k}&\sqrt{\ell+1-k}\\
-\sqrt{\ell+1-k}&\sqrt{k}\end{pmatrix}

(C.43)

and denote its matrix entries by
R^{\ell}_{ab}(k)
where
a,b\in\{0,1\}
, we can express
C_{\ell}
as follows:

C_{\ell}:=\sum_{i=0}^{1}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\Big\lparen R^{\ell}_{i0}(j+i)|{j+i-1}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus R^{\ell}_{i1}(j+i)|{j+i}\rangle_{\ell+2}\Big\rparen\langle{i}|_{2}\otimes\langle{j}|_{\ell+1},

(C.44)

where the subscripts indicate the dimension of each state,

cf.

eq.

C.40
.
Since
R^{\ell}_{00}(0)=0
, the term with
|{i+j-1}\rangle_{\ell}
vanishes when
i=j=0
, so we do not need to worry about interpreting the resulting state
|{-1}\rangle_{\ell}
.
Similarly,
R^{\ell}_{01}(\ell+1)=0
, so we can also ignore
|{\ell}\rangle_{\ell}
.
It follows from
R^{\ell}(k)R^{\ell}(k)^{\mathsf{T}}=I_{2}
that
C_{\ell}C_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}=I_{2\ell+2}
, meaning that
C_{\ell}
is orthogonal and hence unitary.

This formula can be obtained from the known expressions of Clebsch–Gordan coefficients in the special case when one of the representations is the defining (
i.e.
,
\ell=1
) representation,
see
[
Boh93
, p. 173]
,
[
Vil78
, p. 187]
,
[
VK91
, p. 512]
.
We have adapted them to the case when the defining representation is in the first register instead of the second, and for using
\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
instead of
\{-\ell/2,\dotsc,\ell/2\}
to label the standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1}
.
Finally, we are using a slightly different phase convention in
eq.

C.43
to match with our derivations in
section

D.5
.

For completeness, in
section

D.5
we provide a self-contained derivation of Clebsch–Gordan transform based on a decomposition of
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)
.

C.7
Dual Clebsch–Gordan transform

Let us denote the
dual
of the defining representation
T^{1}(M)=M
by

M^{*}:=(M^{-1})^{\mathsf{T}}.

(C.45)

Note that if
U
is unitary then
U^{-1}=U^{\dagger}
so
U^{*}=(U^{\dagger})^{\mathsf{T}}=\overline{U}
.
The
dual
Clebsch–Gordan transformation
D_{\ell}
provides a decomposition of
M^{*}\otimes T^{\ell}(M)
analogous to
eq.

C.41
.

Let us derive a formula for
D_{\ell}
in terms of
C_{\ell}
. First, we can relate
M^{*}
and
M
by noting that

\Bigg[\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}^{*}\Bigg]^{\mathsf{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\frac{1}{ad-bc}\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\
-c&a\end{pmatrix}.

(C.46)

Hence, for any invertible
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

\det(M)\,M^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}M\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}^{\dagger}.

(C.47)

This allows expressing
M^{*}\otimes T^{\ell}(M)
in terms of
M\otimes T^{\ell}(M)
:

\det(M)\big\lparen M^{*}\otimes T^{\ell}(M)\big\rparen=\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\otimes I_{\ell+1}\right]\cdot\big\lparen M\otimes T^{\ell}(M)\big\rparen\cdot\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\otimes I_{\ell+1}\right]^{\dagger}.

(C.48)

To obtain the desired decomposition of
M^{*}\otimes T^{\ell}(M)
, we can first undo the undesired basis change and then apply the regular Clebsch–Gordan transform
C_{\ell}
.
In other words, let

D_{\ell}:=C_{\ell}\cdot\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\otimes I_{\ell+1}\right],

(C.49)

where the
2\times 2
matrix is the same as
S
in
eq.

A.24
and hence satisfies
S(-S)=I_{2}
.
Then, according to
eq.

C.41
,

D_{\ell}\det(M)\big\lparen M^{*}\otimes T^{\ell}(M)\big\rparen D^{\dagger}_{\ell}=T^{1,\ell-1}(M)\oplus T^{0,\ell+1}(M).

(C.50)

After cancelling
\det(M)
from both sides, we get the following dual of
eq.

C.41
:

D_{\ell}\big\lparen M^{*}\otimes T^{\ell}(M)\big\rparen D^{\dagger}_{\ell}=T^{0,\ell-1}(M)\oplus T^{-1,\ell+1}(M),

(C.51)

which justifies calling
D_{\ell}
the
dual Clebsch–Gordan transform
. Equivalently, similar to
eq.

C.42
, we can also express this in terms of
\lambda
and
Q_{\lambda}
:

D_{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\big\lparen M^{*}\otimes Q_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}(M)\big\rparen D^{\dagger}_{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}=Q_{(\lambda_{1}-1,\lambda_{2})}(M)\oplus Q_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}-1)}(M),

(C.52)

which holds for any
\lambda\mathbin{\vdash}n
.
Noting that, for any
i\in\{0,1\}
,

\langle{i}|\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}=(-1)^{i}\langle{1-i}|,

(C.53)

we can use
eq.

C.49
to modify
eq.

C.44
and obtain the following explicit formula for
D_{\ell}
:

D_{\ell}=\sum_{i=0}^{1}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\Big\lparen R^{\ell}_{i0}(j+i)|{j+i-1}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus R^{\ell}_{i1}(j+i)|{j+i}\rangle_{\ell+2}\Big\rparen(-1)^{i}\langle{1-i}|_{2}\otimes\langle{j}|_{\ell+1}.

(C.54)

Appendix D
Symmetric subspace

In this appendix, we introduce the
\ell
-qubit symmetric subspace and discuss its connections with irreducible representations of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, Clebsch–Gordan transform, and the two extremal unitary-covariant channels
\Phi^{\ell}_{1}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{2}
introduced in
section

4.2
which correspond to partial trace and universal
\mathrm{NOT}
.

For any
\ell\geq 0
, we denote the
\ell
-qubit
symmetric subspace
by

\mathrm{Sym}(\ell):=\spn\big\{|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle:w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}\big\}\subseteq\lparen\mathbb{C}^{2}\rparen^{\otimes\ell},

(D.1)

where
|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle
denotes the
\ell
-qubit symmetric state of Hamming weight
w
, see
eq.

B.2
.

D.1
Projector onto the symmetric subspace

Since
\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|{s_{\ell}(w^{\prime})}\rangle=\delta_{ww^{\prime}}
, the set

\big\{|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle:w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}\big\}

is in fact an orthonormal basis for the
\ell
-qubit symmetric subspace, hence the projector onto this subspace is given by

\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}:=\sum_{w=0}^{\ell}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|.

(D.2)

There are several equivalent ways of writing this projector.

Lemma D.1
.

For any
\ell\geq 0
, the following three expressions for the projector
\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}
onto the
\ell
-qubit symmetric subspace are equivalent:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}:=\sum_{w=0}^{\ell}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|=\frac{1}{\ell!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}P(\pi)=(\ell+1)\int\big\lparen|{\psi}\rangle\langle{\psi}|\big\rparen^{\otimes\ell}\,d\psi,

(D.3)

where
|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle
are the symmetric states from
eq.

B.2
,

P(\pi)\in\mathrm{U}(2^{\ell})
are the
\ell
-qubit permutations defined in
eq.

2.1
,
and
d\psi
is the uniform measure on the Bloch sphere, see
eq.

2.4
.

Proof.

Let us denote the above expressions by
\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}
,
\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}^{\prime}
, and
\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.
We will evaluate their matrix entries and show that they are equal.

Let us start with
\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}
.
Note from
eq.

B.2
that, for any
h,w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
,

\langle{0^{\ell-h}1^{h}}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle=\delta_{hw}\binom{\ell}{w}^{-1/2}.

(D.4)

More generally, for any
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
,

\langle{x}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|{y}\rangle=\delta_{|x|w}\delta_{|y|w}\binom{\ell}{w}^{-1},

(D.5)

so we conclude that

\langle{x}|\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}|{y}\rangle=\sum_{w=0}^{\ell}\langle{x}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|{y}\rangle=\delta_{|x||y|}\binom{\ell}{|x|}^{-1}.

(D.6)

To evaluate the matrix entries of the second expression, note that

\langle{x}|\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}^{\prime}|{y}\rangle=\langle{x}|\frac{1}{\ell!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}P(\pi)|{y}\rangle=\delta_{|x||y|}\frac{(\ell-|x|)!|x|!}{\ell!}=\delta_{|x||y|}\binom{\ell}{|x|}^{-1}.

(D.7)

The second equality holds since permutations preserve Hamming weight, and there are
|x|!
ways to map the ones of
x
to those of
y
, and
(\ell-|x|)!
ways to map the zeroes of
x
to those of
y
.

For the third expression, let
|{\psi}\rangle=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
and note that

\langle{x}|\cdot|{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes\ell}=\alpha^{\ell-|x|}\beta^{|x|}

(D.8)

for any
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.
Hence,

\langle{x}|\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}^{\prime\prime}|{y}\rangle=(\ell+1)\int\langle{x}|\cdot|{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes\ell}\,\langle{\psi}|^{\otimes\ell}\cdot|{y}\rangle\,d\psi=(\ell+1)\int\alpha^{\ell-|x|}\beta^{|x|}\bar{\alpha}^{\ell-|y|}\bar{\beta}^{|y|}\,d\psi.

(D.9)

Recall from
eq.

2.2
that we can take

\alpha:=\cos\frac{\theta}{2}
and

\beta:=e^{i\varphi}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}
,
and from
eq.

2.4
that the uniform measure on the Bloch sphere is

d\psi:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\varphi
.
Putting everything together,

\langle{x}|\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}^{\prime\prime}|{y}\rangle=\frac{\ell+1}{4\pi}\int_{\theta=0}^{\pi}\left\lparen\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\right\rparen^{2\ell-|x|-|y|}\left\lparen\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\right\rparen^{|x|+|y|}\sin\theta\,d\theta\int_{\varphi=0}^{2\pi}e^{i\varphi(|x|-|y|)}\,d\varphi.

(D.10)

The second integral evaluates to
2\pi\delta_{|x||y|}
, so we are left with

\langle{x}|\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}^{\prime\prime}|{y}\rangle=\delta_{|x||y|}\frac{\ell+1}{2}\int_{\theta=0}^{\pi}\left\lparen\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\right\rparen^{2\ell-2|x|}\left\lparen\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\right\rparen^{2|x|}\sin\theta\,d\theta=\delta_{|x||y|}\binom{\ell}{|x|}^{-1}.

(D.11)

We are done since the matrix entries in
eqs.

D.6
,
D.7
and
D.11
agree for all
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.
∎

D.2
Symmetric subspace and irreducible representations of
\mathrm{SU}(2)

Here we provide an alternative way of deriving the irreducible representations
T^{\ell}
of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
described in
section

C.1
. The following lemma shows that the matrix entries of
T^{\ell}(M)
, for any
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, can be obtained by restricting the action of
M^{\otimes\ell}
to the
\ell
-qubit symmetric subspace.

Lemma D.2
.

For any integer
\ell\geq 0
, indices
j,k\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
, and matrix
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

\displaystyle M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}t^{\ell}_{jk}(M)|{s_{\ell}(j)}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle{s_{\ell}(j)}|M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle

\displaystyle=t^{\ell}_{jk}(M),

(D.12)

where
t^{\ell}_{jk}(M)
are the matrix entries of
T^{\ell}(M)
, see
eq.

C.9
, and
|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle
is the symmetric state of Hamming weight
w
, see
eq.

B.2
.

Proof.

Since
\langle{s_{\ell}(j)}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle=\delta_{jk}
, we can obtain the second identity from the first one by multiplying both sides with
\langle{s_{\ell}(j)}|
.

To prove the first identity, first note that we can express the sum of all
\ell
-qubit standard basis states of Hamming weight
k\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\\
\lvert x\rvert=k\end{subarray}}|{x}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\ell-k)!k!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}P(\pi)|{0}\rangle^{\otimes\ell-k}|{1}\rangle^{\otimes k}=\binom{\ell}{k}\;\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\left\lparen|{0}\rangle^{\otimes\ell-k}|{1}\rangle^{\otimes k}\right\rparen,

(D.13)

where
\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}
denotes the projector onto the symmetric subspace, see
eq.

D.2
.
Adjusting the normalization, we can write the symmetric state
|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle
of Hamming weight
k
as follows:

|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle=\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\left[\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}|{0}\rangle^{\otimes\ell-k}|{1}\rangle^{\otimes k}\right].

(D.14)

Note how the above equation is similar to
eq.

C.4
. Here the standard basis vectors
|{0}\rangle
and
|{1}\rangle
play a similar role to the variables
x
and
y
of
\psi^{\ell}_{k}(x,y)
. To establish a more formal correspondence, consider the following vector, which is a function of variables
x,y\in\mathbb{C}
:

|{v(x,y)}\rangle:=\big\lparen x|{0}\rangle+y|{1}\rangle\big\rparen^{\otimes\ell}=\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}x^{\ell-\lvert a\rvert}y^{\lvert a\rvert}|{a}\rangle.

(D.15)

Since
|{v(x,y)}\rangle\in\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)
, we have

\langle{v(x,y)}|\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}=\langle{v(x,y)}|
.
Using this and
eq.

D.14
,

\langle{v(x,y)}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle=\langle{v(x,y)}|\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}|{0}\rangle^{\otimes\ell-k}|{1}\rangle^{\otimes k}=\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}x^{\ell-k}y^{k}=\psi^{\ell}_{k}(x,y),

(D.16)

establishing a formal correspondence between the symmetric states
|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle
and the functions
\psi^{\ell}_{k}
defined in
eq.

C.4
.

Let us now consider the action of
M^{\otimes\ell}
on
|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle
. For concreteness, let
M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
. Then

M|{0}\rangle=a|{0}\rangle+c|{1}\rangle
and

M|{1}\rangle=b|{0}\rangle+d|{1}\rangle
,
which is analogous to how
M
acts on the arguments of functions
\psi
in
eq.

C.2
.
Since
\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}
is a linear combination of permutations, see
lemma

D.1
, it commutes with
M^{\otimes\ell}
and thus

M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle=\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\left[\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}\big\lparen a|{0}\rangle+c|{1}\rangle\big\rparen^{\otimes\ell-k}\big\lparen b|{0}\rangle+d|{1}\rangle\big\rparen^{\otimes k}\right],

(D.17)

which is analogous to the left-hand side of
eq.

C.6
.

To obtain an analogue of the right-hand side, note that
M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle
is symmetric under permutations and thus
M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle\in\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)=\spn\big\{|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle:w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}\big\}
. Hence,

M^{\otimes\ell}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\tilde{t}^{\ell}_{jk}(M)|{s_{\ell}(j)}\rangle,

(D.18)

for some coefficients
\tilde{t}^{\ell}_{jk}(M)\in\mathbb{C}
. Combining this with
eqs.

D.14
and
D.17
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\left[\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}\big\lparen a|{0}\rangle+c|{1}\rangle\big\rparen^{\otimes\ell-k}\big\lparen b|{0}\rangle+d|{1}\rangle\big\rparen^{\otimes k}\right]=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\tilde{t}^{\ell}_{jk}(M)\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\left[\binom{\ell}{j}^{1/2}|{0}\rangle^{\otimes\ell-j}|{1}\rangle^{\otimes j}\right].

Multiplying both sides by
\langle{v(x,y)}|
, we recover
eq.

C.6
. Comparing the coefficients at
x^{\ell-j}y^{j}
we conclude that
t^{\ell}_{jk}(M)=\tilde{t}^{\ell}_{jk}(M)
.
∎

D.3
Symmetric subspace and tensor power states

Fix any
\ell\geq 0
and let
V^{\ell}\in\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1},\lparen\mathbb{C}^{2}\rparen^{\otimes\ell})
denote the isometry from
\ell+1
dimensions to the
\ell
-qubit symmetric subspace
\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)
:

\displaystyle V^{\ell}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle\langle{k}|.

(D.19)

The following proposition shows that this isometry relates the states
|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1}
defined in
eq.

C.15
to the tensor power states
|{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes\ell}\in\lparen\mathbb{C}^{2}\rparen^{\otimes\ell}
.

Proposition D.3
.

For any integer
\ell\geq 0
and any vector
|{\psi}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

V^{\ell}|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle=|{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes\ell},

(D.20)

where
|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1}
is the vector defined in
eq.

C.15
.

Proof.

For concreteness, let
|{\psi}\rangle=\begin{pmatrix}a\\
c\end{pmatrix}
.
Recall from
eqs.

C.4
and
C.15
that

|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle:=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}a^{\ell-k}c^{k}|{k}\rangle.

(D.21)

By applying
V^{\ell}
from
eq.

D.19
and substituting the definition of the symmetric state
|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle
from
eq.

B.2
, we find that

\displaystyle V^{\ell}|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{k}^{1/2}a^{\ell-k}c^{k}|{s_{\ell}(k)}\rangle

(D.22)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}a^{\ell-k}c^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\\
\lvert x\rvert=k\end{subarray}}|{x}\rangle

(D.23)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}a^{\ell-\lvert x\rvert}c^{\lvert x\rvert}|{x}\rangle

(D.24)

\displaystyle=|{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes\ell},

(D.25)

as desired.
∎

Let
\Sigma^{\ell}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1},\lparen\mathbb{C}^{2}\rparen^{\otimes\ell})
denote the quantum channel corresponding to the isometry
V^{\ell}
:

\Sigma^{\ell}(\rho):=V^{\ell}\rho V^{\ell\dagger},

(D.26)

for all
\rho\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1})
.
Then the states
|{\psi}\rangle
,
|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle
, and
|{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes\ell}
are related as follows.

Corollary D.4
.

For any integer
\ell\geq 0
and any vector
|{\psi}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

|{\psi}\rangle\langle{\psi}|\overset{T^{\ell}}{\longmapsto}|{\psi^{\ell}}\rangle\langle{\psi^{\ell}}|\overset{\Sigma^{\ell}}{\longmapsto}\lparen|{\psi}\rangle\langle{\psi}|\rparen^{\otimes\ell},

(D.27)

where
T^{\ell}
and
\Sigma^{\ell}
are the maps defined by
eqs.

C.9
and
D.26
, respectively.

Proof.

This follows by combining
D.3
and
C.1
.
∎

D.4
Invariant subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)

Fix an integer
\ell\geq 1
and let

\mathcal{V}:=\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)=\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\spn\{|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle:w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell\}\}

(D.28)

be the subspace of
\ell+1
qubits where the first qubit is arbitrary but the last
\ell
are symmetric. We would like to decompose this as a direct sum of the subspace where
all

\ell+1
qubits are symmetric and its orthogonal complement.

Let us denote the symmetric subspace of
\ell+1
qubits by

\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}:=\mathrm{Sym}(\ell+1)=\spn\{|{s_{\ell+1}(w)}\rangle:w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell+1\}\}.

(D.29)

Using the definition in
eq.

B.2
, we can recursively relate the symmetric states on
\ell+1
qubits to those on
\ell
qubits:

\displaystyle|{s_{\ell+1}(w+1)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\binom{\ell+1}{w+1}^{-1/2}\left[\binom{\ell}{w+1}^{1/2}|{0}\rangle|{s_{\ell}(w+1)}\rangle+\binom{\ell}{w}^{1/2}|{1}\rangle|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle\right]

(D.30)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\ell-w}{\ell+1}}|{0}\rangle|{s_{\ell}(w+1)}\rangle+\sqrt{\frac{w+1}{\ell+1}}|{1}\rangle|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle.

(D.31)

This holds for
w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell-1\}
, but the identity also makes sense when
w=-1
or
w=\ell
since the coefficients of the undefined terms
|{s_{\ell}(-1)}\rangle
and
|{s_{\ell}(\ell+1)}\rangle
vanish.

Let us define another set of unit vectors by simply exchanging the two coefficients in
eq.

D.31
and adding a minus sign to the second term:

|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle:=\sqrt{\frac{w+1}{\ell+1}}|{0}\rangle|{s_{\ell}(w+1)}\rangle-\sqrt{\frac{\ell-w}{\ell+1}}|{1}\rangle|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle

(D.32)

where
w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell-1\}
(we will justify the notation
|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle
in
lemma

D.6
).
We denote the span of these vectors by

\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}:=\spn\{|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle:w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell-1\}\}.

(D.33)

Lemma D.5
.

The subspaces defined in
eqs.

D.28
,
D.29
and
D.33
satisfy

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle=2(\ell+1),

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}

\displaystyle=\ell,

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}

\displaystyle=\ell+2,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}.

(D.34)

Proof.

Since
\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|{s_{\ell}(v)}\rangle=\delta_{wv}
, it is evident from
eqs.

D.28
and
D.29
that
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}
have the claimed dimensions. Since all terms in
|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle
have Hamming weight
w+1
, we conclude that
\langle{s^{\prime}_{\ell}(w)}|{s^{\prime}_{\ell}(v)}\rangle=\delta_{wv}
and hence
\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}
also has the claimed dimension.

It is evident from
eqs.

D.31
and
D.32
that
|{s_{\ell+1}(w)}\rangle
and
|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle
are in
\mathcal{V}
, so
\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}
are subspaces of
\mathcal{V}
. By construction of
|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle
,

\langle{s_{\ell+1}(w+1)}|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle=0,

(D.35)

for any
w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell-1\}
.
More generally, for any
w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell+1\}
and
v\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell-1\}
,

\langle{s_{\ell+1}(w)}|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(v)}\rangle=0

(D.36)

since the two states have different Hamming weights when
w\neq v+1
. This means the subspaces
\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}
are orthogonal to each other. Since
\dim\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}+\dim\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}=\ell+(\ell+2)=\dim\mathcal{V}
, the union of
\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}
constitutes the whole of
\mathcal{V}
and we indeed get a direct sum.
∎

The following lemma provides an alternative formula for the vectors
|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle
defined in
eq.

D.32
. They can be obtained by symmetrizing the
(\ell-1)
-qubit symmetric state
|{s_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle
and one qubit of the singlet state
|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle
. This also explains why
|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle
has the same parameters as a symmetric state, even though it is an
(\ell+1)
-qubit state of Hamming weight
w+1
.

Lemma D.6
.

For any integer
\ell\geq 1
and
w\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell-1\}
,

|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{2\ell}{\ell+1}}\big\lparen I_{2}\otimes\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\big\rparen|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle|{s_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle,

(D.37)

where
\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}:=\frac{1}{\ell!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{\ell}}P(\pi)
is the projector onto the
\ell
-qubit symmetric subspace, see
eq.

D.2
.

Proof.

Substituting the definition of the symmetric state
|{s_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle
from
eq.

B.2
, we can rewrite the right-hand side of
eq.

D.37
as follows:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2\ell}{\ell+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\ell!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}(I_{2}\otimes P(\pi))\frac{|{01}\rangle-|{10}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}|{s_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle

(D.38)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{\ell+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\ell!}\left[|{0}\rangle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}P(\pi)|{1}\rangle|{s_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle-|{1}\rangle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}P(\pi)|{0}\rangle|{s_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle\right]

(D.39)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{\ell+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\ell!}\binom{\ell-1}{w}^{-1/2}\hskip-10.00002pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell-1}\\
\lvert x\rvert=w\end{subarray}}\Bigg[|{0}\rangle\underbrace{\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}P(\pi)|{1}\rangle|{x}\rangle}_{|{h_{1}}\rangle}-|{1}\rangle\underbrace{\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}P(\pi)|{0}\rangle|{x}\rangle}_{|{h_{0}}\rangle}\Bigg].

(D.40)

For each
i\in\{0,1\}
, the expression for
|{h_{i}}\rangle
is symmetric and of Hamming weight
w+i
. This means that
|{h_{i}}\rangle\propto|{s_{\ell}(w+i)}\rangle
. To determine the proportionality constant, we need to count how many times each string appears in the symmetrization. Since permuting
0
’s and
1
’s among themselves does not change the string, we simply need to insert appropriate factorials to account for this. For any
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell-1}
of Hamming weight
\lvert x\rvert=w
,

\displaystyle|{h_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}P(\pi)|{1}\rangle|{x}\rangle=\underbrace{(w+1)!}_{1\text{'s}}\underbrace{(\ell-w-1)!}_{0\text{'s}}\binom{\ell}{w+1}^{1/2}|{s_{\ell}(w+1)}\rangle,

(D.41)

\displaystyle|{h_{0}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{\ell}}P(\pi)|{0}\rangle|{x}\rangle=\underbrace{\phantom{(\!\!}w!}_{1\text{'s}}\underbrace{(\ell-w)!}_{0\text{'s}}\binom{\ell}{w}^{1/2}|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle.

(D.42)

Note that neither of
|{h_{i}}\rangle
depends on
x
, so substituting them back into
eq.

D.40

we get

\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{\ell+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\ell!}\binom{\ell-1}{w}^{1/2}\Big[|{0}\rangle|{h_{1}}\rangle-|{1}\rangle|{h_{0}}\rangle\Big]=\sqrt{\frac{w+1}{\ell+1}}|{0}\rangle|{s_{\ell}(w+1)}\rangle-\sqrt{\frac{\ell-w}{\ell+1}}|{1}\rangle|{s_{\ell}(w)}\rangle,

which agrees with
eq.

D.32
.
∎

The following lemma derives the matrix entries of
M^{\otimes\ell+1}
in the subspaces
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}
and relates them to the representations
T^{\ell+1}
and
T^{\ell-1}
, respectively. In particular, this justifies the notation
|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(w)}\rangle
for the basis of
\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}
.

Lemma D.7
.

For any
M\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
and
\ell\geq 1
, the entries of the matrix
M^{\otimes\ell+1}
restricted to the subspaces
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}
are as follows:

\displaystyle\langle{s_{\ell+1}(j)}|M^{\otimes\ell+1}|{s_{\ell+1}(k)}\rangle

\displaystyle=t^{\ell+1}_{jk}(M),

\displaystyle\forall j,k\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell+1\},

(D.43)

\displaystyle\langle{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(j)}|M^{\otimes\ell+1}|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(k)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\det(M)t^{\ell-1}_{jk}(M),

\displaystyle\forall j,k\in\{0,\dotsc,\ell-1\},

(D.44)

where
t^{\ell}_{jk}(M)
are the entries of the irreducible representation
T^{\ell}
defined in
eq.

C.9
.

Proof.

The first identity follows immediately from
eqs.

D.29
and
D.2
. For the second identity we use
lemma

D.6
:

M^{\otimes\ell+1}|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(k)}\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{2\ell}{\ell+1}}\big\lparen I_{2}\otimes\Pi_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\big\rparen\Big\lparen M^{\otimes 2}|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle\otimes M^{\otimes\ell-1}|{s_{\ell-1}(k)}\rangle\Big\rparen.

(D.45)

Note that
(M\otimes M)|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle=\det(M)|{\Psi^{-}}\rangle
and

M^{\otimes\ell-1}|{s_{\ell-1}(k)}\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}t^{\ell-1}_{jk}(M)|{s_{\ell}(j)}\rangle

according to
eq.

D.18
. Thus

M^{\otimes\ell+1}|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(k)}\rangle=\det(M)\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}t^{\ell-1}_{jk}(M)|{s^{\prime}_{\ell}(j)}\rangle.

(D.46)

Recalling that
\langle{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(j)}|{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(j^{\prime})}\rangle=\delta_{jj^{\prime}}
, we get the desired formula by multiplying both sides by
\langle{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(j)}|
.
∎

D.5
Symmetric subspace and (dual) Clebsch–Gordan transform

The decomposition of the space
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)
as
\mathcal{V}_{\Tr}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}
is closely related to Clebsch–Gordan transform. Similar to the map
C_{\ell}
from
eq.

C.40
, consider a map

C_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)\to\mathbb{C}^{\ell}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\ell+2}

(D.47)

defined by stacking the vectors
\langle{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(v)}|
and
\langle{s_{\ell+1}(w)}|
as rows of a matrix:

C_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}=\sum_{v=0}^{\ell-1}\big\lparen|{v}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus 0_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\langle{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(v)}|+\sum_{w=0}^{\ell+1}\big\lparen 0_{\ell}\oplus|{w}\rangle_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\langle{s_{\ell+1}(w)}|,

(D.48)

where
0_{\ell}
denotes the
\ell
-dimensional all zeroes column vector.
Substituting the formulas for
\langle{s^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(v)}|
and
\langle{s_{\ell+1}(w)}|
from
eqs.

D.32
and
D.31
, we get

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v=0}^{\ell-1}\big\lparen|{v}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus 0_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\left\lparen\sqrt{\frac{v+1}{\ell+1}}\langle{0}|\langle{s_{\ell}(v+1)}|-\sqrt{\frac{\ell-v}{\ell+1}}\langle{1}|\langle{s_{\ell}(v)}|\right\rparen

(D.49)

\displaystyle+\sum_{w=0}^{\ell+1}\big\lparen 0_{\ell}\oplus|{w}\rangle_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\left\lparen\sqrt{\frac{\ell+1-w}{\ell+1}}\langle{0}|\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|+\sqrt{\frac{w}{\ell+1}}\langle{1}|\langle{s_{\ell}(w-1)}|\right\rparen.

We can rearrange the terms in each sum to pull out
\langle{s_{\ell}(v)}|
and
\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|
, respectively:

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v=0}^{\ell}\left\lparen\sqrt{\frac{v}{\ell+1}}\big\lparen|{v-1}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus 0_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\langle{0}|-\sqrt{\frac{\ell-v}{\ell+1}}\big\lparen|{v}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus 0_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\langle{1}|\right\rparen\langle{s_{\ell}(v)}|

(D.50)

\displaystyle+\sum_{w=0}^{\ell}\left\lparen\sqrt{\frac{\ell+1-w}{\ell+1}}\big\lparen 0_{\ell}\oplus|{w}\rangle_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\langle{0}|+\sqrt{\frac{w+1}{\ell+1}}\big\lparen 0_{\ell}\oplus|{w+1}\rangle_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\langle{1}|\right\rparen\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|.

We can now combine the two sums into a single sum:

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}=\sum_{w=0}^{\ell}\Biggl[

\displaystyle\left\lparen\sqrt{\frac{w}{\ell+1}}|{w-1}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus\sqrt{\frac{\ell+1-w}{\ell+1}}|{w}\rangle_{\ell+2}\right\rparen\langle{0}|

(D.51)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\lparen-\sqrt{\frac{\ell-w}{\ell+1}}|{w}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus\sqrt{\frac{w+1}{\ell+1}}|{w+1}\rangle_{\ell+2}\right\rparen\langle{1}|\Biggr]\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|.

We can simplify this even further by introducing another sum to group the two terms together:

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}=\sum_{i=0}^{1}\sum_{w=0}^{\ell}\left\lparen R^{\ell}_{i0}(w+i)|{w+i-1}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus R^{\ell}_{i1}(w+i)|{w+i}\rangle_{\ell+2}\right\rparen\langle{i}|\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|

(D.52)

where

R^{\ell}(k):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell+1}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{k}&\sqrt{\ell+1-k}\\
-\sqrt{\ell+1-k}&\sqrt{k}\end{pmatrix}

(D.53)

is the same matrix as in
eq.

C.43
.
Note that
eq.

D.52
is identical to
eq.

C.44
for
C_{\ell}
, except for the input space being
\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)
instead of
\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1}
.
In other words, the two maps are related as follows:

C_{\ell}=C_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\cdot\left[I_{2}\otimes V^{\ell}\right]

(D.54)

where
V^{\ell}
is the isometry from
eq.

D.19
.

Similar to
eq.

C.49
, the
dual
of
eq.

D.52
is given by

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}:={}

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\cdot\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\otimes I_{2}^{\otimes\ell}\right]

(D.55)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{1}\sum_{w=0}^{\ell}\left\lparen R^{\ell}_{i0}(w+i)|{w+i-1}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus R^{\ell}_{i1}(w+i)|{w+i}\rangle_{\ell+2}\right\rparen(-1)^{i}\langle{1-i}|\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|,

(D.56)

which is identical to
eq.

C.54
, except for the input space being
\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)
instead of
\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1}
.
This constitutes a self-contained derivation of Clebsch–Gordan transform and its dual.

Let
S:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
denote the
2\times 2
matrix in
eq.

D.55
.
Noting that
\langle{0}|S=\langle{1}|
and
\langle{1}|S=-\langle{0}|
, we can derive the following dual version of
eq.

D.49
that will be useful later:

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v=0}^{\ell-1}\big\lparen|{v}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus 0_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\left\lparen\sqrt{\frac{\ell-v}{\ell+1}}\langle{0}|\langle{s_{\ell}(v)}|+\sqrt{\frac{v+1}{\ell+1}}\langle{1}|\langle{s_{\ell}(v+1)}|\right\rparen

(D.57)

\displaystyle+\sum_{w=0}^{\ell+1}\big\lparen 0_{\ell}\oplus|{w}\rangle_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\left\lparen-\sqrt{\frac{w}{\ell+1}}\langle{0}|\langle{s_{\ell}(w-1)}|+\sqrt{\frac{\ell+1-w}{\ell+1}}\langle{1}|\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|\right\rparen.

Appendix E
Implementation of the extremal covariant channels
\Phi^{\ell}_{\Tr}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}

The goal of this appendix is to derive quantum circuits for implementing the channels
\Phi^{\ell}_{\Tr}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}
from
section

4.3
which are used in
section

4.4
.
Recall from
lemma

E.4
that we have identified them with the two extremal
\mathrm{U}(2)
-covariant channels
\Phi^{\ell}_{1}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{2}
, respectively, whose Choi matrices are given by
lemma

4.1
.

Our strategy for deriving quantum circuits for these channels consists of the following steps:
(i) use dual Clebsch–Gordan transform to extract a set of Kraus operators from the Choi matrices of the channel,
(ii) combine the Kraus operators into a Stinespring isomtery, and
(iii) derive a quantum circuit implementing this isometry.

Note that extremal
\mathrm{SU}(2)
-covariant quantum channels have also been studied in
[
Nuw14
,
CKK+22
,
CKJ20
]
.
Some results concerning their structure that are proved here can also be found in these papers.

E.1
Representations of quantum channels

Let us briefly summarize different ways of representing a quantum channel (see
[
Wat18
]
for more background).
By interconverting between these representations, we will find isometries that correspond to the two extremal channels
\Phi^{\ell}_{1}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{2}
.

Recall from
section

2.1
that a quantum channel
\Phi\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})
is fully described by its Choi matrix
J(\Phi)\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
defined in
eq.

2.5
.
Since
J(\Phi)
is positive semidefinite, see
eq.

2.7
, we can find
r=\rank J(\Phi)
vectors
|{k_{1}}\rangle,\dotsc,|{k_{r}}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}
such that

J(\Phi)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}|{k_{i}}\rangle\langle{k_{i}}|.

(E.1)

Let
K_{i}^{\Phi}\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})
be such that
|{K_{i}^{\Phi}}\rangle=|{k_{i}}\rangle
,
i.e.
, the vectorization of
K_{i}^{\Phi}
(see
definition

A.1
) coincides with
|{k_{i}}\rangle
.
Then

\Phi(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}K_{i}^{\Phi}\rho K_{i}^{\Phi\dagger},

(E.2)

for any
\rho\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
.
This is known as
Kraus representation
of
\Phi
and the
K_{i}^{\Phi}
are known as
Kraus operators
.
The map
\Phi
is CPTP if and only if

\sum_{i=1}^{r}K_{i}^{\Phi\dagger}K_{i}^{\Phi}=I_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}.

(E.3)

Another useful way of representing a quantum channel
\Phi\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}})
is by a map
U_{\Phi}\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{r})
defined in terms of Kraus operators of
\Phi
:

U_{\Phi}:=\sum_{i=1}^{r}K_{i}^{\Phi}\otimes|{i}\rangle.

(E.4)

Note that
eq.

E.3
is equivalent to
U_{\Phi}^{\dagger}U_{\Phi}=I_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}
, meaning that
U_{\Phi}
is an isometry.
The action of
\Phi
on any
\rho\in\mathrm{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}})
can be expressed as follows:

\Phi(\rho)=\Tr_{2}\big[U_{\Phi}\rho U_{\Phi}^{\dagger}\big],

(E.5)

which produces the same output as
eq.

E.2
.
This is known as
Stinespring representation
of
\Phi
and the map
U_{\Phi}
is called
Stinespring isometry
.

E.2
Kraus operators of
\Phi^{\ell}_{1}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{2}

Let us find Kraus representations of the two extremal channels
\Phi^{\ell}_{1},\Phi^{\ell}_{2}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1},\mathbb{C}^{2})
from
section

4.2
.
Recall from
eq.

4.4
in
lemma

4.1
that their Choi matrices are

\displaystyle J(\Phi^{\ell}_{1})

\displaystyle=D^{\dagger}_{\ell}\left[\frac{\ell+1}{\ell}I_{\ell}\oplus 0_{\ell+2}\right]D_{\ell},

\displaystyle J(\Phi^{\ell}_{2})

\displaystyle=D^{\dagger}_{\ell}\left[0_{\ell}\oplus\frac{\ell+1}{\ell+2}I_{\ell+2}\right]D_{\ell},

(E.6)

where
D_{\ell}
denotes dual Clebsch–Gordan transform, see
section

C.7
.
Similar to
eq.

D.54
, we can express
D_{\ell}
as follows:

D_{\ell}=D_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}\cdot\left[I_{2}\otimes V^{\ell}\right],

(E.7)

where
V^{\ell}
is the isometry defined in
eq.

D.19
that acts as
\langle{s_{\ell}(w)}|V^{\ell}=\langle{w}|
.
Using this identity, we can convert
eq.

D.57
from
D_{\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)}
to
D_{\ell}
:

\displaystyle D_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v=0}^{\ell-1}\big\lparen|{v}\rangle_{\ell}\oplus 0_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\left\lparen\sqrt{\frac{\ell-v}{\ell+1}}\langle{0}|\langle{v}|+\sqrt{\frac{v+1}{\ell+1}}\langle{1}|\langle{v+1}|\right\rparen

(E.8)

\displaystyle+\sum_{w=0}^{\ell+1}\big\lparen 0_{\ell}\oplus|{w}\rangle_{\ell+2}\big\rparen\left\lparen-\sqrt{\frac{w}{\ell+1}}\langle{0}|\langle{w-1}|+\sqrt{\frac{\ell+1-w}{\ell+1}}\langle{1}|\langle{w}|\right\rparen.

Following the procedure in
eq.

E.1
, we can find Kraus operators of the channels
\Phi^{\ell}_{1}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{2}
from the spectral decomposition of their Choi matrices in
eq.

E.6
:

\displaystyle J(\Phi^{\ell}_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in[\ell]}|{K_{v}^{\Tr}}\rangle\langle{\overline{K_{v}^{\Tr}}}|,

\displaystyle J(\Phi^{\ell}_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in[\ell+2]}|{K_{w}^{\mathrm{UNOT}}}\rangle\langle{\overline{K_{w}^{\mathrm{UNOT}}}}|,

(E.9)

where the Kraus operators
K_{v}^{\Tr}
and
K_{w}^{\mathrm{UNOT}}
are derived below.
The reason we denote them like this is because eventually in
lemma

E.4
we will identify
\Phi^{\ell}_{1}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{2}
with the partial trace and universal NOT channels
\Phi^{\ell}_{\Tr}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathrm{UNOT}}
, which were introduced in
definition

4.3
.

Lemma E.1

(Kraus operators for
\Phi^{\ell}_{1}
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{2}
)
.

For any integer
\ell\geq 1
